Observations and synthesis

- The substrate/electrode niche for implantables is already served by specialists like DYCONEX/MST with ISO 13485 credentials and implantable material stacks, plus CDMOs like Cirtec that integrate power (solid‑state batteries), packaging, and assembly. LGIT meets or exceeds the patterning and packaging density bar, but lacks visible ISO 13485 medical certifications and implantable materials track record in the provided sources — a primary gap to remedy for immediate credibility ([DYCONEX](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [FDA QMSR](https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/postmarket-requirements-devices/quality-management-system-regulation-qmsr)).
- Strategic partnerships are a proven pathway: GlucoTrack’s OEM selection of Cirtec underscores how clinical‑stage innovators prefer CDMOs that bring implantable microelectronics, power, and assembly under one roof. LGIT should target similar arrangements — supplying validated flexible substrates/electrode modules into a CDMO’s implantable platform — to accelerate entry while building its own ISO 13485/QMSR medical line in parallel ([BioSpace, 2024](https://www.biospace.com/glucotrack-selects-cirtec-medical-as-manufacturer-for-its-implantable-continuous-blood-glucose-monitor); [ProactiveInvestors, 2026](https://www.proactiveinvestors.com/companies/news/1048513/ilika-ships-first-commercial-electrode-batch-to-cirtec-medical-1048513.html)).
- CGM offers a pragmatic beachhead: The CGM market’s growth and device architecture (thin flexible sensor substrates, low‑power comms) align with LGIT’s BLE module and substrate know‑how; the regulatory path for subcutaneous sensors and external wearables is typically faster than chronic neurostim implants. LGIT can credibly target “CGM sensor substrate” and “flexible wearable interposer/antenna module” BOM slots first ([PRNewswire – BLE](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/lg-innotek-develops-the-worlds--smallest-bluetooth-low-energy-module-301092828.html); [ClinicalTrialsArena, 2026](https://www.clinicaltrialsarena.com/news/glucotrack-eyes-human-trials-for-implantable-blood-glucose-monitor)).

B. Cross‑topic insights: partnerships, geographies, litigation, acquisitions

Review-site sentiment and complaints (proxy analysis)

- There are no G2/Capterra‑style reviews for component/CDMO vendors in the provided sources. Instead, OEM awards, trade‑show positioning, and regulatory conformance serve as reputation proxies. Cirtec’s selection by GlucoTrack and its electrode partnership with Ilika, MST/DYCONEX’s implantable profile, and Resolution/Argon’s ISO‑certified facilities present “trust signals” for OEM buyers. FDA’s QMSR harmonization will further push OEMs to select ISO 13485‑aligned partners with demonstrated component traceability and robust purchasing controls ([BioSpace, 2024](https://www.biospace.com/glucotrack-selects-cirtec-medical-as-manufacturer-for-its-implantable-continuous-blood-glucose-monitor); [ProactiveInvestors, 2026](https://www.proactiveinvestors.com/companies/news/1048513/ilika-ships-first-commercial-electrode-batch-to-cirtec-medical-1048513.html); [DYCONEX](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [FDA QMSR](https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/postmarket-requirements-devices/quality-management-system-regulation-qmsr)).

Competitor geographic footprints

- Cirtec: U.S. base; active at MD&M West (Anaheim), with cross‑Atlantic component supply partners (Ilika in the UK). This suggests healthy North America and Europe linkages ([MDO, 2026](https://www.medicaldesignandoutsourcing.com/mdm-west-preview-anaheim-medtech-suppliers-cmos-vendors/); [ProactiveInvestors, 2026](https://www.proactiveinvestors.com/companies/news/1048513/ilika-ships-first-commercial-electrode-batch-to-cirtec-medical-1048513.html)).
- MST/DYCONEX: Switzerland/Germany/USA footprint, long‑standing in implantables — strong Europe/North America capacity blend ([Medical Device Network – MST](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/mst-group/)).
- EMS leaders: Global (Jabil, Flex, Sanmina, Celestica, Nortech). Deep Asia/NA/EU coverage implied; no detailed breakdown in sources ([Mordor Intelligence](https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/electronic-manufacturing-services-for-healthcare-and-medical-device-market)).
- Argon: Four U.S. sites (IL, TX, WI, NY) for vertically integrated contract manufacturing ([MDO – 2025/2026](https://www.medicaldesignandoutsourcing.com/mdm-west-2025-preview-medtech-suppliers-anaheim-show/)).
- Resolution: U.S. (Minnesota) FDA‑registered facility and cleanrooms ([MDO – 2025/2026](https://www.medicaldesignandoutsourcing.com/mdm-west-2025-preview-medtech-suppliers-anaheim-show/)).
- LG Innotek: HQ Korea; sales subsidiaries in Taipei, Shanghai, Tokyo, San Jose, Detroit, Chicago; expanding package substrates capacity — underpins global commercial reach and manufacturing investment potential ([PRNewswire – BLE module “About LG Innotek”](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/lg-innotek-develops-the-worlds--smallest-bluetooth-low-energy-module-301092828.html); [KED Global, 2023](https://www.kedglobal.com/tech,-media-telecom/newsView/ked202301300020)).

Partnerships and ecosystem integrations

- Cirtec’s relationships (GlucoTrack; Ilika electrodes) demonstrate an integrated implantable platform play, combining microelectronics, power, and assembly — a future template for LGIT collaborations in CGM and neurostim ([BioSpace, 2024](https://www.biospace.com/glucotrack-selects-cirtec-medical-as-manufacturer-for-its-implantable-continuous-blood-glucose-monitor); [ProactiveInvestors, 2026](https://www.proactiveinvestors.com/companies/news/1048513/ilika-ships-first-commercial-electrode-batch-to-cirtec-medical-1048513.html)).
- MST/DYCONEX integrates implantable HDI with chip packaging and primary batteries (Litronik) — a “stack” that OEMs value. LGIT can partner upstream (materials and substrates) or downstream (module sub‑assemblies) to enter this stack ([Medical Device Network – MST](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/mst-group/)).
- LG Innotek’s non‑medical integrations (autonomous/robotics sensing; 5G satellite module) show competence in system‑level modules — relevant when adding BLE, antennas, and micro‑packages to medical subassemblies for CGMs and neurostim controllers ([PRNewswire UK, 2025](https://prnewswire.co.uk/news/lg-innotek); [Korea Times, 2026](https://www.koreatimes.co.kr/www/tech/2026/01/133_373653.html)).

Litigation, IP dispute, or antitrust history

- No material disputes were identified in the provided sources for Cirtec, MST/DYCONEX, Jabil/Flex/Sanmina/Celestica, Argon, or Resolution.
- The dataset includes examples of broader device recalls (Stryker LIFEPAK 1000) and FDA enforcement frameworks (QMSR, MDR) relevant to supplier risk; it also includes non‑sector IP litigation headlines (e.g., Longford/Arigna) not tied to these competitors. The lesson is supplier due diligence; OEMs will avoid vendors without robust QMSR/ISO 13485 documentation and postmarket vigilance programs ([FDA – LIFEPAK 1000 recall](https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/scripts/cdrh/cfdocs/cfres/res.cfm?id=214086); [FDA – Overview of Device Regulation](https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/device-advice-comprehensive-regulatory-assistance/overview-device-regulation)).

Acquisitions and capacity moves

- Cirtec: Strengthened implantable platform via Ilika supply agreement for solid‑state battery electrodes (commercial supply arrangement announced January 2026; initial batch shipped March 2026), suggesting scaling of implantable energy solutions in parallel with electrode/substrate assemblies ([ProactiveInvestors, 2026](https://www.proactiveinvestors.com/companies/news/1048513/ilika-ships-first-commercial-electrode-batch-to-cirtec-medical-1048513.html)).
- LG Innotek: Investing ~$1.1B to ramp substrate/camera module capacity and gearing up FC‑BGA mass production; package solutions now a major profit contributor — building the scale needed for medical programs when coupled with ISO 13485 adoption ([KED Global, 2023](https://www.kedglobal.com/tech,-media-telecom/newsView/ked202301300020); [Korea Times, 2026](https://www.koreatimes.co.kr/www/tech/2026/01/133_373653.html)).
- Broader EMS/CDMO landscape: Investing in inspection and early engineering capabilities to meet miniaturization and quality demands — an industry‑wide arms race that LGIT must match for medical customers ([Mordor Intelligence – EMS healthcare](https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/electronic-manufacturing-services-for-healthcare-and-medical-device-market)).

C. Strategic implications for LG Innotek

Defensible roles in the medical device supply chain

- Near‑term (12–24 months): Component supplier for medical‑grade flexible substrates and electrode interposers
  - Focus on CGM sensor substrates (subcutaneous; semi‑implantable) and wearable flexible interposers/antennas, where LGIT’s 10‑µm fine patterning, micro‑vias, and BLE module experience translate directly. Partner with an ISO 13485‑certified CDMO (e.g., Cirtec, MST/DYCONEX for implantables; Resolution or Argon for wearables and catheterized assemblies) to co‑validate material stacks, biocompatible surface finishing, and sterilization compatibility. This approach accelerates entry while building internal QMSR conformance ([PRNewswire – BLE](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/lg-innotek-develops-the-worlds--smallest-bluetooth-low-energy-module-301092828.html); [MDO – CDMO profiles](https://www.medicaldesignandoutsourcing.com/mdm-west-preview-anaheim-medtech-suppliers-cmos-vendors/); [DYCONEX](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).
- Mid‑term (24–48 months): Module supplier for neurostimulation leads and early BCI electrode substrates
  - As ISO 13485/QMSR systems harden, co‑develop thin‑film electrode modules (arrays, feedthrough interposers, lead bodies) with implantable CDMOs or select OEMs. Leverage LGIT’s materials innovation (e.g., precious metal‑free substrate learnings) to propose stabilized, lower‑cost, or ESG‑advantaged interconnect stacks. Offer system‑in‑package options that integrate sensing, BLE/antenna, and power management for external controllers or rechargeable neurostim can‑tethers ([ReadMagazine – smart IC substrate](https://readmagazine.com/industries/semiconductor-electronics/lg-innotek-debuts-next-gen-smart-ic-substrate); [MDO – Cirtec specialization](https://www.medicaldesignandoutsourcing.com/mdm-west-preview-anaheim-medtech-suppliers-cmos-vendors/)).

Go‑to‑market and capability roadmap

1) Quality and regulatory foundation
- Implement ISO 13485 across a segregated medical line aligned with FDA QMSR (supplier controls, DMR/DHR, risk management per ISO 14971, software validation where applicable). Prepare for OEM audits and for‑cause inspections; align labeling/MDR responsibilities and complaint handling in supplier agreements ([FDA QMSR](https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/postmarket-requirements-devices/quality-management-system-regulation-qmsr); [FDA – Overview of Device Regulation](https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/device-advice-comprehensive-regulatory-assistance/overview-device-regulation)).
- Establish cleanroom assembly for sensitive modules (ISO Class 7–8) and sterilization‑aware process development (EtO/EO compatibility, gamma/e‑beam exposure studies for polymer stacks). CDMO exemplars (Resolution, MGS) demonstrate expected facility capabilities ([MDO – 2025/2026](https://www.medicaldesignandoutsourcing.com/mdm-west-2025-preview-medtech-suppliers-anaheim-show/)).

2) Technology and materials integration
- Co‑develop implant‑grade stackups with partners experienced in long‑term in‑body reliability (e.g., DYCONEX/MST for HDI PCBs/electrodes; Cirtec for implantable assemblies). Validate interconnect stability (moisture ingress, electrochemical stress), conductor adhesion, parylene/PVDF/PI passivation, and platinum‑group electrode interfaces where needed ([DYCONEX – Medtech implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).
- Extend LGIT’s BLE/wireless modules into medical subassemblies (e.g., CGM readers, patch controllers) with rigorous RF emissions/EMC control — leveraging the company’s differentiated RF and antenna design abilities cited in the BLE module announcement ([PRNewswire – BLE](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/lg-innotek-develops-the-worlds--smallest-bluetooth-low-energy-module-301092828.html)).

3) Partnerships and pilots
- Mirror the Ilika–Cirtec model: sign a co‑development/supply agreement with a battery innovator or electrode‑materials startup to bundle LGIT substrates with next‑gen power or sensing layers for implantables; pilot with one CGM OEM (e.g., CBGM entrants) and one neuromodulation OEM (or CDMO) to produce design history files and validation packages suitable for OEM design controls ([ProactiveInvestors – Ilika/Cirtec](https://www.proactiveinvestors.com/companies/news/1048513/ilika-ships-first-commercial-electrode-batch-to-cirtec-medical-1048513.html); [BioSpace – GlucoTrack/Cirtec](https://www.biospace.com/glucotrack-selects-cirtec-medical-as-manufacturer-for-its-implantable-continuous-blood-glucose-monitor)).

4) Commercial strategy
- Target BOM line items where value is high and commoditization low: implantable electrode arrays/interposers; CGM sensor substrates (with integrated traces/micro‑vias and bonding pads); flexible hermetic feedthrough interposers; wearable BLE/RF interposers with medical coatings. Offer “design‑to‑manufacture” support that large EMS/CDMOs market today to reduce OEM NPI risk ([Mordor Intelligence – EMS early engineering](https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/electronic-manufacturing-services-for-healthcare-and-medical-device-market)).

Risk considerations and mitigations

- Certification gap: Without ISO 13485/QMSR alignment, OEMs will limit LGIT to non‑critical prototypes. Fast‑track certification and hire experienced medical QA/RA leadership; consider auditing against AAMI TIR102 mapping to ensure CFR 21 coverage under ISO 13485 ([FDA QMSR – resources](https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/postmarket-requirements-devices/quality-management-system-regulation-qmsr)).
- Materials/biocompatibility risk: Implantables demand validated polymers, adhesives, and conductor stacks; co‑develop with implantable specialists (DYCONEX/MST) and run GLP‑compliant biocompatibility testing where indicated (per ISO 10993 series; not in dataset, but implied common practice). Use sterilization and aging studies upfront ([DYCONEX – implant context](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).
- Commercial risk: Compete with entrenched incumbents; differentiate via density, cost/ESG (precious‑metal‑free concepts), and speed. Leverage LGIT’s scale in FC‑BGA to guarantee supply (tight through 2027) and negotiate volume commitments with OEMs/CDMOs ([KED Global – FC‑BGA outlook](https://www.kedglobal.com/tech,-media-telecom/newsView/ked202301300020); [ReadMagazine – substrate innovation](https://readmagazine.com/industries/semiconductor-electronics/lg-innotek-debuts-next-gen-smart-ic-substrate)).

D. Implications by target segment

1) CGM systems

- Opportunity: Sensor substrates (flexible PI with Au traces and micro‑vias), BLE/RF interposers, reader interconnects. GlucoTrack’s implantable CBGM and timeline to first‑in‑human create a near‑term window for substrate/module suppliers; Cirtec is already involved at the system level for GlucoTrack, indicating that partnering with Cirtec (tier‑1 CDMO) to supply subassemblies could be the fastest route ([ClinicalTrialsArena, 2026](https://www.clinicaltrialsarena.com/news/glucotrack-eyes-human-trials-for-implantable-blood-glucose-monitor); [BioSpace, 2024](https://www.biospace.com/glucotrack-selects-cirtec-medical-as-manufacturer-for-its-implantable-continuous-blood-glucose-monitor)).
- LGIT fit: Fine‑patterned substrate capability and BLE competence; needs medical QMS and material validations. Offer substrate modules with integrated strain relief and bonding features to reduce OEM assembly risk ([PRNewswire – BLE](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/lg-innotek-develops-the-worlds--smallest-bluetooth-low-energy-module-301092828.html)).

2) Neurostimulation

- Opportunity: Spinal cord stim/electrode leads; cranial nerve stimulation; peripheral nerve interfaces; remote programming is expanding (Biotronik’s study), but electrodes/interconnects remain the performance bottleneck for chronic reliability — an area where implantable‑grade flexible substrates can add value ([Biotronik SCS study, 2026](https://www.implantable-device.com/2026/01/13/biotronik-study-reveals-utility-of-remote-monitoring-and-remote-programming-in-scs/); [Implantable‑device.com – Pain OEM density](https://www.implantable-device.com/category/conditions/pain/)).
- LGIT fit: Provide electrode interposers, flexible lead substrates, and sealed interconnects; explore module‑in‑package solutions for external controllers (power/comm). Partner with established implantable CDMOs (Cirtec; MST/DYCONEX) and pursue OEM pilots with SCS players seeking next‑gen leads.

3) Brain‑computer interfaces

- Opportunity: High‑channel flexible micro‑electrode arrays, ultra‑high‑density interposers, hermetic feedthroughs; early‑stage clinical devices (Neuralink/Synchron) imply stringent biocompatibility and stability requirements and an appetite for partners who can push density/reliability limits ([Implantable‑device.com – entries including Neuralink/Synchron](https://www.implantable-device.com/category/conditions/pain/)).
- LGIT fit: Longer‑term. Pursue feasibility with research‑to‑translation groups via a CDMO that already serves investigational implants. Differentiate via 10‑µm patterning, stable thin‑film stacks, and integrated RF/power for external modules.

E. Recommended action plan for LG Innotek

- 0–6 months:
  - Appoint medical QA/RA leadership; define scope of ISO 13485/QMSR medical line. Begin gap assessment against FDA QMSR and ISO 13485; plan cleanroom infrastructure. Build supplier control program aligned to medical purchasing controls ([FDA QMSR](https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/postmarket-requirements-devices/quality-management-system-regulation-qmsr)).
  - Initiate technical workshops with Cirtec and MST/DYCONEX to scope co‑validation projects for CGM substrates and neurostim interposers. Adopt their implantable materials stack guidance to accelerate biocompatibility and sterilization readiness ([ProactiveInvestors – Ilika/Cirtec](https://www.proactiveinvestors.com/companies/news/1048513/ilika-ships-first-commercial-electrode-batch-to-cirtec-medical-1048513.html); [DYCONEX](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).
- 6–18 months:
  - Execute pilot builds of CGM sensor substrates and BLE/RF interposers in a segregated medical environment. Generate IQ/OQ/PQ packages, DHRs, and traceability artifacts. Pursue one OEM alpha build (e.g., clinical‑stage CGM startup) via a CDMO channel partner ([BioSpace – GlucoTrack/Cirtec](https://www.biospace.com/glucotrack-selects-cirtec-medical-as-manufacturer-for-its-implantable-continuous-blood-glucose-monitor)).
  - Start feasibility on neurostim electrode interposers with a CDMO and one OEM; focus on accelerated life testing under moisture/electrochemical stress consistent with implant scenarios ([DYCONEX](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).
- 18–36 months:
  - Achieve ISO 13485 certification for medical line; present QMSR conformance during OEM/CDMO audits. Move CGM substrates and wearable modules to low‑rate initial production; add module‑in‑package offerings for controllers using LGIT’s BLE/RF know‑how ([FDA QMSR](https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/postmarket-requirements-devices/quality-management-system-regulation-qmsr); [PRNewswire – BLE](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/lg-innotek-develops-the-worlds--smallest-bluetooth-low-energy-module-301092828.html)).
  - Win first neurostim electrode substrate design‑in for a next‑gen lead or cranial array; continue BCI feasibility studies.

Opinionated assessment

Given the evidence, LG Innotek’s strongest near‑term competitive position is as a specialist supplier of medical‑grade flexible substrates/electrode interposers and as a module partner for BLE/RF interposers in CGM and neurostimulation system controllers. The company’s track record in ultra‑fine patterning, micro‑vias, and system‑in‑package RF aligns with core unmet needs in thin CGM sensor stacks and compact neurostim/wearable electronics. However, absent visible ISO 13485/QMSR credentials and implantable material validations, LGIT should not attempt to go direct to implantable OEMs with finished electrode arrays at first. Instead, it should leverage high‑trust CDMOs (e.g., Cirtec; MST/DYCONEX) as channel multipliers while rapidly maturing a dedicated medical manufacturing/QMS foundation. Sustained investment in ESG‑oriented substrate materials (e.g., precious‑metal‑free plating innovations) could become a pricing and sustainability differentiator in volume programs over the next 3–5 years, especially in CGM wearables and high‑mix neurostim cables/modules.

- MD&M West exhibitor rosters include multiple CDMOs and component specialists across neuromodulation and active implantables (Cirtec, Resolution, Argon, Meddux, Rose Medical, etc.), documenting a dense partner ecosystem into which LGIT can plug its substrate capabilities without reinventing downstream assembly and sterilization at the outset ([MDO – 2025/2026 previews](https://www.medicaldesignandoutsourcing.com/mdm-west-preview-anaheim-medtech-suppliers-cmos-vendors/); [MDO – 2025 previews](https://www.medicaldesignandoutsourcing.com/mdm-west-2025-preview-medtech-suppliers-anaheim-show/)).
- The implantable device repository highlights the breadth of neuromodulation OEMs — from legacy giants (Medtronic, Boston Scientific) to growth players (Nevro) and BCIs (Neuralink, Synchron) — validating long‑term demand for high‑reliability flexible interconnects and electrode arrays ([Implantable‑device.com – Pain category](https://www.implantable-device.com/category/conditions/pain/)).

### MR-07: Buying Process, Budget & Pricing [+]

**Status:** success | **Time:** 1695.8s | **Sources:** 245

Title: Entering MedTech Substrate Supply: Buying Process, Budget Dynamics, and Pricing Landscape for Advanced Flexible Substrates and Electrode Modules in CGM, Neurostimulation, and BCI

Abstract
This report analyzes the enterprise B2B buying process, budget structure, and pricing/selection dynamics for advanced medical device substrates, flexible circuit components, and electrode modules used in wearable and implantable devices across Continuous Glucose Monitoring (CGM), neurostimulation, and brain–computer interface (BCI) segments in North America and Europe (with a global expansion lens). It triangulates vendor white papers, regulatory/reimbursement documentation, market research synopses, and engineering literature to identify the decision-making unit (DMU), approval chains, budget holders, procurement cycles, selection criteria, pricing norms, and commercial realities. The report further assesses where a high-density interconnect substrate leader like LG Innotek (LGIT)—with 10 µm-class chip‑on‑film and flexible PCB capability in IT/auto but without medical certifications or biosignal IP—can defensibly enter the medical supply chain, using companies such as DYCONEX (Micro Systems Technologies), CorTec, NeuroNexus, and leading EMS/CDMOs as benchmarks. The analysis highlights concrete near-term entry points (non-implant external CGM transmitter flex/interposers, patch interconnects, antennas) and a staged path toward implantable electrode substrates, detailing required quality, biocompatibility, and regulatory steps (ISO 13485, IPC Class III, traceability, sterilization compatibility, soak/accelerated life testing, noble‑metal electrode finishes, and protective coatings). Evidence is documented using APA in-text citations with source links and a consolidated reference list. Where quantitative benchmarks (e.g., CAC, churn, DSO) are not publicly available in the provided sources, this is stated explicitly. ([Medical Device Network/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/dyconex1/); [MedDeviceOnline/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.meddeviceonline.com/doc/biocompatible-flexible-substrates-for-directly-implantable-applications-0002); [Medical Design Briefs LCP substrates](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/32810-using-lcp-based-flexible-substrates-for-medical-smart-sensor-applications); [Market Prospects – medical PCB demands](https://www.market-prospects.com/articles/the-new-role-of-pcbs-in-smart-medical-devices); [Federal Register – neurostim CPT codes](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/07/16/2025-13271/medicare-and-medicaid-programs-cy-2026-payment-policies-under-the-physician-fee-schedule-and-other); [PR Newswire – CGM market players/partnerships](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html); [NeuroNexus – BRAIN platform](https://braininitiative.nih.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ppp_neuronexus_high_def_electrodes_508c.pdf); [ResearchAndMarkets – flexible implantable electrode market](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOorsUWfpkV4twVdQwfGzbEpp_aechZZ-87xVUw5Xd99CPyuiPh2p); [Future Market Insights – implantable neurostimulator electrodes](https://www.futuremarketinsights.com/reports/implantable-neurostimulator-electrode-market); [Frontiers – materials/biocompatibility](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/nanotechnology/articles/10.3389/fnano.2025.1686599/full); [Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs for med devices](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices); [EMSG – medical PCB manufacturing](https://emsginc.com/industries-we-serve/medical-industry/); [CO-AX – medical electronics manufacturing](https://coaxinc.com/markets/medical-electronics/); [GMI Solutions – medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare); [FlexiPCB – certifications](https://flexipcb.com); [InsightPCB – standards/certifications](https://www.insightpcb.com/flexible-rigid-flex-pcbs); [BioRxiv – flexible PCB potentiostat for wearable glucose monitoring](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.02.24.707657v1.full.pdf))

Introduction: Why Flexible Substrates and Electrode Modules Matter in CGM, Neurostimulation, and BCI
Across CGM, neurostimulation, and BCI, the shift toward thin, conformal, high‑density flexible electronics and electrode arrays is central to device miniaturization, comfort, signal integrity, and biocompatibility. On the implantable side, established substrates (polyimide, LCP) with noble-metal thin films and specialized electrode finishes (Pt, PtIr, IrO2) underpin chronic stability; on the wearable side, flexible circuit integration enables low-profile sensing, amplification, and telemetry. Vendors like DYCONEX (Micro Systems Technologies) explicitly position biostable LCP/PI thin-film interconnects with recessed/raised electrode options and ISO 13485/IPC Class III manufacturing for long‑term implants in neural and cardiac applications, setting an industry benchmark for substrate suppliers aspiring to medical-grade participation ([Medical Device Network/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/dyconex1/); [MedDeviceOnline/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.meddeviceonline.com/doc/biocompatible-flexible-substrates-for-directly-implantable-applications-0002)). In parallel, academic and translational work continues to validate flexible PCB platforms for glucose monitoring, highlighting integration paths for flexible circuits into biosensing front ends and potentiostats in textile/wearable formats ([BioRxiv flexible PCB potentiostat](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.02.24.707657v1.full.pdf)).

Reimbursement and clinical adoption indicators underscore demand durability. For example, U.S. CMS rulemaking codifies ongoing procedural reimbursement for neurostimulator analysis and programming (e.g., CPT 95970, 95976, 95977), including attention to evolving hypoglossal nerve stimulation cost inputs, which signals an active and economically supported therapy ecosystem drawing sustained component demand ([Federal Register – CPT 95970/95976/95977 excerpts](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/07/16/2025-13271/medicare-and-medicaid-programs-cy-2026-payment-policies-under-the-physician-fee-schedule-and-other)).

CGM’s industrial structure is likewise consolidated yet partnership‑oriented. Market reports cite Abbott, Dexcom, Medtronic, and GlySens as leading players, with notable integrations like Dexcom–Ypsomed (closed-loop system) and Abbott–Insulet (sensor–pump AID integration) illustrating a buying preference for proven modules and interoperable components, which creates defined entry points for certified flex substrate and electrode module suppliers who can slot into OEM product architectures ([PR Newswire – CGM competitive insights and partnerships](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html)).

On the neurotechnology supplier roster, specialized electrode companies (CorTec, NeuroNexus, Microprobes) and precision substrate houses (DYCONEX) serve as canonical incumbents. NeuroNexus’s NIH BRAIN exhibits show configurable high‑definition arrays with multi‑channel stimulation/recording platforms for investigational clinical use—emblematic of the performance and documentation expectations for electrode vendors in clinical/near‑clinical programs ([NeuroNexus BRAIN platform exhibits](https://braininitiative.nih.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ppp_neuronexus_high_def_electrodes_508c.pdf)). Market compendia further identify broader competitive sets for flexible implantable electrodes, including Abbott, Blackrock Neurotech, Inomed, CorTec, Plexon, Ripple Neuro, Synapse Biomedical, Neuralink, and others, mapping a fragmented yet credential‑heavy landscape for implantable interconnect and electrode supply ([ResearchAndMarkets – flexible implantable electrode market, companies](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOorsUWfpkV4twVdQwfGzbEpp_aechZZ-87xVUw5Xd99CPyuiPh2p)).

Methodology and Source Notes
This report synthesizes publicly available vendor white papers, engineering articles, regulatory and reimbursement texts, and market research synopses provided in the source pack. The most reliable citations are vendor technical white papers from DYCONEX/MST and Medical Design Briefs/SAE Media, U.S. Federal Register rulemaking (CMS), and peer-reviewed/academic outlets; market sizing and company roster material from ResearchAndMarkets/Future Market Insights are used narrowly to frame directional scale and segmentation (with caveats). Where quantitative benchmarks were not present in the provided sources (e.g., CAC, DSO norms, churn), gaps are explicitly stated. ([Medical Device Network/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/dyconex1/); [MedDeviceOnline/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.meddeviceonline.com/doc/biocompatible-flexible-substrates-for-directly-implantable-applications-0002); [Medical Design Briefs LCP substrates](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/32810-using-lcp-based-flexible-substrates-for-medical-smart-sensor-applications); [Federal Register](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/07/16/2025-13271/medicare-and-medicaid-programs-cy-2026-payment-policies-under-the-physician-fee-schedule-and-other); [PR Newswire CGM](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html); [NeuroNexus BRAIN](https://braininitiative.nih.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ppp_neuronexus_high_def_electrodes_508c.pdf); [ResearchAndMarkets](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOorsUWfpkV4twVdQwfGzbEpp_aechZZ-87xVUw5Xd99CPyuiPh2p); [Future Market Insights](https://www.futuremarketinsights.com/reports/implantable-neurostimulator-electrode-market); [Frontiers](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/nanotechnology/articles/10.3389/fnano.2025.1686599/full); [Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices); [EMSG](https://emsginc.com/industries-we-serve/medical-industry/); [CO-AX](https://coaxinc.com/markets/medical-electronics/); [GMI Solutions](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare); [FlexiPCB](https://flexipcb.com); [InsightPCB](https://www.insightpcb.com/flexible-rigid-flex-pcbs); [BioRxiv](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.02.24.707657v1.full.pdf))

A. Buying Process and Decision-Making Unit (DMU)

A1. Typical buying cycle length by segment
- Neurostimulation/BCI implantables: For implantable electrode substrates and interconnects, buying cycles are prolonged due to design assurance, biocompatibility verification, sterilization compatibility, process validation, and clinical/regulatory dependencies. Vendor documentation emphasizes accelerated life testing, IPC Class III/IIIA compliance, ISO 13485 traceability, and sterilization compatibility as prerequisites—each requiring qualification time, audits, and engineering iterations. Practically, this translates to multi‑quarter to multi‑year evaluation/procurement cycles tied to device program gates (feasibility → design verification → design validation → clinical builds → commercialization). DYCONEX highlights Class III requirements, accelerated lifetime testing, and full traceability—signals of long validation paths before release to serial supply ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Medical Device Network/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/dyconex1/)). Medical Design Briefs advises that ECMs/EMS should be engaged early for DFM reviews, audits, and process capability assessments, reinforcing that supplier selection is embedded in upstream design cycles rather than late-stage tactical sourcing ([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices)).

- CGM/wearables (external/transdermal subsystems): External transmitter flexes, adhesive patch interconnects, and flexible antennas are still regulated but avoid chronic implantation requirements; supplier selection can align to device refresh cycles and platform extensions. However, given CGM OEM partnership models (e.g., Dexcom–Ypsomed, Abbott–Insulet) and emphasis on reliability and cost, evaluation remains rigorous, with supplier QMS and process audits common prior to AVL approval. Buying cycles are typically shorter than implantables but still multi-quarter as they tie into NPI windows and coordinated product/partner timelines ([PR Newswire – CGM partnerships](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html)).

- Research/clinical-stage BCI: Clinical/research BCI projects often source investigational arrays from specialists like NeuroNexus under investigational-use contexts; cycles are driven by study timelines and sponsor approvals, with bespoke designs configured per protocol—again implying quarters to years rather than weeks. NeuroNexus’s BRAIN platform underscores investigational status and sponsor approvals, consistent with extended evaluation paths ([NeuroNexus BRAIN exhibits](https://braininitiative.nih.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ppp_neuronexus_high_def_electrodes_508c.pdf)).

Evidence indicates there is no “fast” enterprise cycle for implantables; even for external wearables, supplier onboarding, design transfers, and QMS audits introduce multi-quarter timelines. The sources do not provide numeric medians; thus, time-to-close ranges are presented qualitatively. ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices); [PR Newswire – CGM partnerships](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html); [NeuroNexus BRAIN exhibits](https://braininitiative.nih.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ppp_neuronexus_high_def_electrodes_508c.pdf))

A2. DMU size and role composition
The DMU for substrate/electrode module sourcing in regulated medtech is cross‑functional and formal. Recurring roles include:

- R&D/Design Engineering (Electrical, Materials, Packaging)
- Manufacturing Engineering/Process Development
- Quality Assurance/Quality Engineering (QA/QE)
- Regulatory Affairs/Clinical (esp. for implantables)
- Sourcing/Strategic Procurement
- Supplier Quality/Incoming Inspection
- Operations/Planning
- Executive Program Management; occasionally Business Unit GM/VP for final signoff

Medical Design Briefs emphasizes early engineering collaboration (DFM), production/testing capability checks, and ongoing reviews of yields/field data, implying involvement from engineering, quality, and supply chain leadership. EMS/CDMOs marketing (EMSG, CO‑AX, GMI) stress ISO 13485, FDA registration (where applicable), process validation, and traceability, indicating that Supplier Quality and Regulatory interface deeply with vendor qualification. Implantables (Class III-type) add sterilization/materials/biocompatibility experts. ([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices); [EMSG medical PCB](https://emsginc.com/industries-we-serve/medical-industry/); [CO‑AX medical electronics](https://coaxinc.com/markets/medical-electronics/); [GMI medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare); [DYCONEX – traceability/Class III](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).

Table A2.1. Typical DMU composition for advanced medical substrates/electrodes

| Role cluster | Typical titles | Core responsibilities |
|---|---|---|
| R&D/Design | Director/Manager Electrical Eng.; Principal Materials Engineer; Packaging Engineer | Define electrical/mechanical specs; material stacks (PI/LCP); electrode finishes; reliability targets |
| Manufacturing/Process | Director/Manager Manufacturing Eng.; NPI Lead | Assess manufacturability; process capability; DFM; pilot to scale-up; sterilization flow |
| Quality/Supplier Quality | Director/Manager QA/QE; SQE | QMS audits; IPC Class III conformance; incoming inspection plans; PPAP/FAI |
| Regulatory/Clinical | Regulatory Affairs Director; Clinical Engineering | Assess biocompatibility (ISO 10993 via supplier data), sterilization compatibility, labeling/UDI impacts |
| Procurement/Sourcing | Category Manager; Strategic Sourcing Director | Commercial terms; NRE/volume pricing; multi-source risk; contract terms |
| Program leadership | VP/GM BU; Program Director | Milestone approvals; budget signoff; partner alignment |

([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices); [DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants))

A3. Typical initiator/champion titles
- For external CGM subsystems: Electrical/Hardware Engineering Managers, Platform Leads, and Sourcing Category Managers often initiate supplier scouting for flex interposers, antennas, and patch connectors to meet cost/miniaturization targets within the next platform cycle. The Dexcom–Ypsomed and Abbott–Insulet integrations exemplify engineering-led partner scouting to enable closed‑loop architectures—implicitly requiring compatible substrates/interconnect elements and triggering early supplier engagement ([PR Newswire – CGM partnerships](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html)).

- For implantables: Principal Materials Engineers, Neurostimulation System Architects, and Supplier Quality leaders co‑champion, given the criticality of material systems, chronic stability, and QMS evidence. DYCONEX’s emphasis on material systems, sterilization compatibility, and accelerated lifetime testing signals material/vendor specialists as champions for implant‑grade interconnects ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).

A4. Typical evaluators/blockers
- Supplier Quality Engineering (SQE) and Quality Assurance (QA) are primary technical/compliance evaluators and can block vendors over missing ISO 13485 certification, inadequate IPC Class III/IIIA evidence, lack of biocompatibility/sterilization compatibility documentation, or insufficient traceability/capability validation. DYCONEX’s marketing directly addresses these criteria (Class III standards, validated processes, traceability), underscoring their blocker status in the DMU ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Medical Device Network/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/dyconex1/)).

- Regulatory Affairs can block implant‑adjacent or implant‑direct components if supplier materials/coatings lack biocompatibility records (e.g., ISO 10993 testing via the OEM or supplier dossiers) or if sterilization methods compromise material integrity. Market Prospects notes medical PCBs require protective coatings (e.g., Parylene) and must pass environmental durability testing to mitigate patient risk—a regulatory concern that can halt qualification ([Market Prospects – medical PCBs: coatings/testing](https://www.market-prospects.com/articles/the-new-role-of-pcbs-in-smart-medical-devices)).

- Program Management/Finance can block for schedule risk if vendor cannot align to NPI/clinical build windows; Medical Design Briefs stresses throughput/capability alignment to program needs, implying schedule-based blocks are common ([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices)).

A5. Budget-holder and executive-approver titles
- Budget holders typically include BU Program Directors and Operations/Supply Chain leadership for tooling/NRE and COGS line items; executive approvers often include the BU VP/GM and VP Operations/Sourcing for entering new strategic suppliers (especially for implant-class components). EMS/CDMO providers highlight full product/box build and traceable assembly services under ISO 13485/FDA registration, suggesting budget interfaces with Operations and Supply Chain leaders who own make/buy and supplier awards ([CO‑AX medical electronics](https://coaxinc.com/markets/medical-electronics/); [EMSG medical PCB](https://emsginc.com/industries-we-serve/medical-industry/); [GMI medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare)).

A6. Approval chain stages and common failure points
- Stages: (1) Technical discovery/DFM reviews; (2) QMS and process audits (ISO 13485, IPC Class III, traceability, sterilization compatibility); (3) Prototype/Pilot qualification (FAI/PPAP-like documentation, reliability testing); (4) Commercial/legal review; (5) AVL/award and ramp; (6) Ongoing quarterly business/quality reviews. Medical Design Briefs describes ongoing manufacturer–OEM collaboration, including biweekly production yield reviews and field data monitoring, consistent with a structured approval and maintenance cycle ([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices)).

- Failure points: (a) Insufficient documentation/traceability; (b) inability to meet Class III/IIIA or sterilization/material requirements; (c) weak accelerated life data for implants; (d) late demonstration of throughput/capability; (e) misalignment to NPI timelines; (f) cost non-competitiveness vs. certified incumbents. DYCONEX’s positioning on accelerated life testing, traceability and Class III standards reflects these historical failure modes and the need to pre‑empt them with robust evidence ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Medical Device Network/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/dyconex1/)).

B. Budget Structure and Commercial Reality

B7. Budget structure norms (OPEX vs CAPEX; centralized vs distributed; recurring vs project)
- For component supply and CDMO services, spend is predominantly project-based and COGS‑linked (OPEX) once in production, preceded by CAPEX/NRE (tooling, fixtures, test development, validation) during development. EMS/CDMOs advertise turnkey support including prototyping to full production and box-build, suggesting mixed NRE plus per‑unit commercial structures that roll into production OPEX/COGS post‑validation ([EMSG medical PCB](https://emsginc.com/industries-we-serve/medical-industry/); [GMI medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare)).

- Budget centralization: For implantables and regulated wearables, budget authority is typically centralized within the BU program’s capital plan and Operations/Supply Chain COGS governance, with Engineering controlling NRE releases and Operations/Procurement controlling recurring spend awards—consistent with the EMS guidance that the ECM should improve buying performance and collaborate on throughput and costs ([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices)).

B8. Budget ownership norms by function
- NRE/CAPEX: R&D Engineering and Program Management typically own NRE release for DFM/proto/validation; Operations/Manufacturing Engineering may own fixtures and test‑equipment CAPEX. EMS/CDMOs’ NPI and engineering collaboration messaging implies engineering/program ownership of early spend ([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices)).

- Recurring COGS/OPEX: Operations/Supply Chain and Strategic Sourcing own supplier awards and recurring unit pricing, with Supplier Quality accountable for ongoing quality cost/PPM targets. EMS/CDMOs emphasize scalable production and logistics, aligning with Operations budget governance ([GMI medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare)).

B9. ACV/ARPA or spend benchmarks by segment
- Public per‑account ACV or ARPA benchmarks are not present in the provided sources. However, category scale indicators contextualize potential spend. For implantable neurostimulator electrodes, Future Market Insights cites a $1.3B market value in 2026, forecast to $2.6B in 2036 at 7% CAGR, suggesting multi‑hundred‑million annual category expenditures across OEMs/institutions globally—a backdrop for multi‑million supplier awards per OEM program at scale, though vendor-specific ACV is not disclosed in sources ([Future Market Insights – implantable neurostimulator electrodes](https://www.futuremarketinsights.com/reports/implantable-neurostimulator-electrode-market)).

- Flexible implantable electrode macro market: ResearchAndMarkets indicates rapid growth from $2.6B (2025) to $2.9B (2026) at ~11.6% historic CAGR, with continued expansion to ~$4.45B by 2030 (11.3% CAGR), signaling robust aggregate spend potential on electrodes and underlying substrates—again without per‑account ACV but indicating a healthy addressable base for qualified suppliers ([ResearchAndMarkets – flexible implantable electrode market](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOorsUWfpkV4twVdQwfGzbEpp_aechZZ-87xVUw5Xd99CPyuiPh2p)).

- CGM context: PR Newswire confirms a concentrated OEM set (Dexcom, Abbott, Medtronic, GlySens) and notes major integrations—implying large, strategically sticky supply awards to a small number of vendors per subsystem (e.g., flex interposers, patch interconnects), but the sources do not quantify ACV per supplier ([PR Newswire – CGM competitive insights](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html)).

B10. Budget-range or line-item benchmarks
- Specific budget bands/line items are not published in the provided sources. However, multiple sources describe necessary cost elements suppliers must incorporate (e.g., Class III build standards, sterilization/material compatibility validation, accelerated life testing, protective coatings like Parylene), which translate into NRE line items (materials qualification, process validation, reliability test campaigns) and recurring costs (specialized materials: LCP/PI, noble metals, coatings). These elements are repeatedly emphasized by DYCONEX and Market Prospects as requirements for medical-grade interconnects, implying their presence in OEM budgets ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Market Prospects – coatings/testing](https://www.market-prospects.com/articles/the-new-role-of-pcbs-in-smart-medical-devices)).

B11. Replacement cycle/refresh cadence
- Implantables: Substrates/electrodes are designed for multi‑year in‑body stability; DYCONEX underscores long‑term biostability and accelerated life testing as part of design assurance, and Medical Design Briefs notes LCP-based embedded die stability after >11 months of soak testing—both consistent with refresh cycles measured in years, often tied to device revisions rather than periodic replacement of electrodes. Replacement occurs upon therapy upgrade or when leads/revisions are clinically indicated, not on a rapid refresh schedule ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Medical Design Briefs LCP substrates](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/32810-using-lcp-based-flexible-substrates-for-medical-smart-sensor-applications)).

- Wearables/CGM: External transmitter and patch interconnect flexes refresh to OEM platform cadence (often 1–3 years), but the sources do not provide numeric cadences; partnership announcements indicate ongoing platform integration cycles across years ([PR Newswire – CGM partnerships](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html)).

B12. Churn benchmarks
- No public vendor churn (logo/revenue) benchmarks are present in the provided sources for this component/CDMO category. The nature of regulated, validated supply (ISO 13485, IPC Class III, sterilization/material qualifications) and the investment in process validation typically produce low churn once awarded; however, this is an inference based on category characteristics rather than quantified by the sources. We therefore flag data as sparse. ([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices); [DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).

B13. CAC benchmarks
- No customer acquisition cost or payback benchmarks are provided in the sources. This category’s CAC is driven by extended technical sales, audits, qualification runs, and regulatory documentation—all time‑ and cost‑intensive—yet not quantified by the provided materials. Data are sparse. ([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices)).

B14. LTV benchmarks
- No quantitative LTV benchmarks are in the sources. Given long program lifecycles and low supplier turnover after validation, implicit LTV is high for awarded suppliers; nevertheless, specific figures are absent. Data are sparse. ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).

B15. Payment terms and DSO norms
- The provided sources do not disclose explicit net terms or DSO norms for component/CDMO transactions in medical devices. EMS/CDMO marketing emphasizes end‑to‑end services, global logistics, and partnership models but does not state terms. Data are sparse. ([GMI medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare); [EMSG medical PCB](https://emsginc.com/industries-we-serve/medical-industry/)).

C. Use Cases, Pricing, and Selection Dynamics

C16. Dominant jobs-to-be-done by segment
- Implantable neurostimulation/BCI: Supply biocompatible, flexible interconnect substrates and electrode modules with noble‑metal thin films and electrode finishes enabling chronic stability, high channel counts, and precise stimulation/recording in spinal cord, cranial, and peripheral targets. NeuroNexus’s arrays for brain/spinal targets and DYCONEX’s LCP/PI noble‑metal thin film substrates characterize the core job-to-be-done: long‑term, high‑reliability, miniaturized electrode interfaces with Class III manufacturing rigor ([NeuroNexus BRAIN platform exhibits](https://braininitiative.nih.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ppp_neuronexus_high_def_electrodes_508c.pdf); [Medical Device Network/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/dyconex1/); [MedDeviceOnline/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.meddeviceonline.com/doc/biocompatible-flexible-substrates-for-directly-implantable-applications-0002)).

- CGM/wearable biosensing: Provide thin, conformal flexible circuits for sensor front ends (e.g., potentiostats), interposers/antennas, and skin-contact interconnects that meet biocompatibility and durability norms while enabling low cost and high volume. The BioRxiv wearable glucose potentiostat demonstrates a concrete integration of flexible PCB systems for glucose detection, underscoring the job of delivering electromechanical integrity in ultrathin form factors ([BioRxiv flexible PCB potentiostat](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.02.24.707657v1.full.pdf)).

C17. Core vs nice-to-have use case split
- Core (Table C17.1):
  - Implantables: chronic biostability, electrode electrochemistry, sterilization compatibility, ISO 13485/QMS/traceability, IPC Class III/IIIA conformance, accelerated life/soak testing, protective coatings if required, fine‑line HDI and reliable vias, and stable material systems (LCP/PI, noble metals). These are non‑negotiable selection gates ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [MedDeviceOnline/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.meddeviceonline.com/doc/biocompatible-flexible-substrates-for-directly-implantable-applications-0002); [Medical Design Briefs LCP substrates](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/32810-using-lcp-based-flexible-substrates-for-medical-smart-sensor-applications)).

  - CGM/wearables: mechanical robustness for daily wear, skin-contact safety, ingress protection where relevant, high yield at volume, and cost competitiveness for consumer‑scale COGS targets. Partnerships cited in CGM reflect the centrality of proven interoperability and cost/performance balance ([PR Newswire – CGM partnerships](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html)).

- Nice-to-have:
  - Modular customization toolkits, rapid prototyping support, and in‑house test fixture development—valuable, but secondary to core regulatory and reliability gates. EMS advisories suggest OEMs value ECMs that “buy smarter and faster” and collaborate closely; while not substitute for compliance, these capabilities can differentiate vendors in tie‑breaks ([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices)).

C18. Pain frequency and ROI evidence
- Pain frequency: In implantables, failures are unacceptable; hence design targets emphasize zero‑failure, Class III build quality, and long‑term stability. DYCONEX highlights IPC Class III/IIIA and accelerated life testing as table stakes, with accelerated validation used to de‑risk long‑term exposure—indirect evidence that avoiding rare but catastrophic failures is the central ROI driver ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).

- ROI: In neurostimulation, durable reimbursement (e.g., CPT 95970/95976/95977) sustains therapy economics and justifies investment in reliable components. Misvaluation reviews in CMS rulemaking point to cost scrutiny but ongoing reimbursement coverage—evidence that ROI rests on functional reliability plus procedural economics. OEMs thus value substrates/electrodes that reduce returns/RMAs, support miniaturization (therapy adoption), and pass audits—translating into avoided costs and preserved revenue streams ([Federal Register – CPT codes and misvaluation process](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/07/16/2025-13271/medicare-and-medicaid-programs-cy-2026-payment-policies-under-the-physician-fee-schedule-and-other)).

C19. Pricing models and reference price anchors
- Component/CDMO pricing models in sources are implied rather than numeric: NRE for DFM, tooling, fixtures, test development; unit pricing for production with volume breaks; potential milestone billing in validation phases. EMS/CDMO pages highlight prototyping through volume production and turnkey box-build—consistent with NRE plus per‑unit commercial structures. No per‑unit reference prices for medical‑grade electrode substrates are disclosed in the provided materials. Data on explicit price anchors is therefore sparse, though ResearchAndMarkets/FMI category sizes provide high‑level anchors for category spend rather than SKU pricing ([EMSG medical PCB](https://emsginc.com/industries-we-serve/medical-industry/); [GMI medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare); [ResearchAndMarkets – market scale](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOorsUWfpkV4twVdQwfGzbEpp_aechZZ-87xVUw5Xd99CPyuiPh2p); [Future Market Insights – market scale](https://www.futuremarketinsights.com/reports/implantable-neurostimulator-electrode-market)).

C20. Preferred purchase channels by buyer type
- Enterprise OEMs buy directly from certified component suppliers and EMS/CDMOs with ISO 13485, FDA registration if needed, and proven medical track records, as underscored by EMSG, CO‑AX, and GMI positioning. Given the regulatory weight, marketplaces/resellers are atypical for implantable‑adjacent components; direct or CDMO‑mediated relationships dominate. Substrate specialists like DYCONEX work closely in co‑development, consistent with direct enterprise engagement ([EMSG medical PCB](https://emsginc.com/industries-we-serve/medical-industry/); [CO‑AX medical electronics](https://coaxinc.com/markets/medical-electronics/); [GMI medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare); [Medical Device Network/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/dyconex1/)).

C21. Seasonality of buying
- The sources do not report seasonality. In practice, medtech procurement aligns to program gates and fiscal calendars; however, absent explicit data, we mark seasonality findings as not available in the provided materials. Data are sparse. ([Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices)).

C22. Top purchase criteria
Recurring selection criteria distilled from sources:

1) Regulatory/Quality: ISO 13485 QMS, IPC Class III/IIIA builds, documented traceability, validated manufacturing processes, and audit readiness—DYCONEX repeatedly highlights these as core differentiators ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).

2) Biocompatibility/Material systems: LCP/PI noble‑metal thin film stacks with electrode coatings (Pt, PtIr, IrO2, Ti); sterilization compatibility; protective coatings like Parylene when needed; Medical Design Briefs (LCP) and Market Prospects articulate material and coating demands for medical PCBs/sensors ([MedDeviceOnline/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.meddeviceonline.com/doc/biocompatible-flexible-substrates-for-directly-implantable-applications-0002); [Medical Design Briefs LCP substrates](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/32810-using-lcp-based-flexible-substrates-for-medical-smart-sensor-applications); [Market Prospects – coatings/testing](https://www.market-prospects.com/articles/the-new-role-of-pcbs-in-smart-medical-devices)).

3) Reliability/Validation: Accelerated life testing, soak testing evidence, bend/flex endurance, sterilization resilience—critical for implants and desirable for wearables; DYCONEX’s accelerated life validation claims are emblematic ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants)).

4) Electrical performance/HDI capability: Ultra‑fine lines/vias; high‑channel density arrays; stable interconnect architectures—key for BCI/neurostim electrodes and compact CGM transmitters; DYCONEX and LCP articles detail ultra‑thin multilayers and fine microvias ([Medical Device Network/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/dyconex1/); [Medical Design Briefs LCP substrates](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/32810-using-lcp-based-flexible-substrates-for-medical-smart-sensor-applications)).

5) Cost and scale: Ability to meet cost targets and scale production; EMS/CDMOs emphasize scalable NPI→volume transitions and global logistics ([GMI medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare)).

6) Ecosystem fit: For CGM, interoperability with partner platforms (e.g., pump integrations) requires mechanical/electrical compatibility and partner co‑development readiness—illustrated by CGM AID integrations ([PR Newswire – CGM partnerships](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html)).

C23. NPS or customer satisfaction benchmarks
- The provided sources do not furnish NPS or satisfaction benchmarks for component vendors. Data are sparse.

C24. Common objections and lost-deal reasons
- Lacking ISO 13485 certification and medical regulatory documentation is a principal objection; DYCONEX’s emphasis on ISO 13485/Class III/traceability directly addresses this barrier, signaling its prevalence. Missing biocompatibility/sterilization evidence and inadequate accelerated life data are also frequent blockers for implantables. Capability/throughput gaps and inability to meet NPI windows produce schedule‑based losses. Cost non‑competitiveness vs. incumbents with proven yields can also stall deals, especially in CGM wearables where COGS pressure is high ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices); [PR Newswire CGM](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html)).

Strategic Implications and Entry Pathways for LG Innotek (LGIT)

Foundational insight: What wins in medical substrate/electrode supply
The most compelling through-line across sources is that medical‑grade substrate/electrode suppliers differentiate on regulated process maturity (ISO 13485; IPC Class III/IIIA), documented material systems (LCP/PI with noble metals and electrode finishes), sterilization compatibility, and rigorous reliability validation (accelerated lifetime/soak, bend/flex endurance), backed by full traceability. DYCONEX’s positioning codifies the benchmark. Engineering literature reinforces material choices (Pt, Au, Ti; PEDOT:PSS; polyimide, PDMS, silk) and the feasibility of ultrathin multilayer LCP structures via standard PCB processes, offering a technology path that aligns well with LGIT’s 10 µm‑class HDI sophistication—provided medical QMS and validation investments are made ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Medical Device Network/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.medicaldevice-network.com/contractors/electronics/dyconex1/); [MedDeviceOnline/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.meddeviceonline.com/doc/biocompatible-flexible-substrates-for-directly-implantable-applications-0002); [Medical Design Briefs LCP substrates](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/32810-using-lcp-based-flexible-substrates-for-medical-smart-sensor-applications); [Frontiers – biocompatible materials](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/nanotechnology/articles/10.3389/fnano.2025.1686599/full)).

Where LGIT can defensibly participate first (near-term)
- External CGM subsystems and wearables: Supply high‑density flexible interposers, transmitter flexes, rigid‑flex interconnects, and flexible antennas for CGM transmitters and skin‑patch connectors. These components are safety‑critical but not chronically implanted, reducing the immediate biocompatibility/sterilization bar relative to implant leads. The wearable glucose potentiostat case and CGM partnership ecosystem suggest robust demand for reliable, thin, high‑volume flex circuits around sensors/transmitters. LGIT’s existing 10 µm HDI capability and mass‑manufacturing heritage fit these jobs, provided it establishes ISO 13485 QMS, IPC Class III builds, and medical-grade documentation/traceability workflows akin to InsightPCB/FlexiPCB’s certifications—steps that are verifiable in supplier marketing and are baseline for medtech AVL inclusion ([BioRxiv flexible PCB potentiostat](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.02.24.707657v1.full.pdf); [PR Newswire – CGM partnerships](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html); [FlexiPCB – ISO 13485/IPC Class 3](https://flexipcb.com); [InsightPCB – IPC-6013/ISO](https://www.insightpcb.com/flexible-rigid-flex-pcbs)).

- Investigational/clinical‑stage arrays with established sponsors: Partner as a build‑to‑print HDI substrate provider to clinical‑stage BCI or peripheral neurostimulation developers under the system integrator’s regulatory umbrella (e.g., supplying noble‑metal thin‑film substrates without owning electrode electrochemistry IP). NeuroNexus’s BRAIN platform and market rosters show multiple players sourcing investigational electrodes; a co‑development path with a credentialed electrode OEM can accelerate LGIT’s learning while avoiding immediate direct implant responsibility. This requires LCP/PI thin‑film process adaptation, electrode finish plating stack capabilities, and joint validation protocols with the prime contractor ([NeuroNexus BRAIN exhibits](https://braininitiative.nih.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ppp_neuronexus_high_def_electrodes_508c.pdf); [ResearchAndMarkets – flexible implantable electrode companies](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOorsUWfpkV4twVdQwfGzbEpp_aechZZ-87xVUw5Xd99CPyuiPh2p)).

Medium‑term (staged toward implantables)
- Transition to implant‑adjacent subassemblies: After ISO 13485 and medical IPC Class III are established and audited, expand into implant‑adjacent flex interconnects used within IPG (implantable pulse generator) enclosures or as replaceable subassemblies subject to sterilization but not chronic tissue contact. Build sterilization compatibility evidence and reliability dossiers progressively, guided by DYCONEX’s documented practices (accelerated life, Class IIIA where applicable), and by Medical Design Briefs’ LCP process guidance ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Medical Design Briefs LCP substrates](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/32810-using-lcp-based-flexible-substrates-for-medical-smart-sensor-applications)).

- Implantable electrode substrates (selective scope): Develop LCP/PI thin‑film stacks with embedded noble‑metal conductors and optional electrode finishes (Pt, PtIr, IrO2, Ti) and demonstrate biocompatibility/soak testing, adhesion/bend endurance, and sterilization resilience. This mirrors DYCONEX’s offerings and requires robust materials science plus Class III documentation. Protective coatings (e.g., Parylene) and electrochemical surface preparation (e.g., roughened Pt, IrOx) should be validated per target therapy. Only after pilot clinical supply under a prime contractor should LGIT consider direct implant OEM contracting ([MedDeviceOnline/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.meddeviceonline.com/doc/biocompatible-flexible-substrates-for-directly-implantable-applications-0002); [Market Prospects – Parylene and testing](https://www.market-prospects.com/articles/the-new-role-of-pcbs-in-smart-medical-devices)).

Build/Buy/Partner recommendations
- Build: Stand up an ISO 13485-certified medical QMS; adopt IPC Class III/IIIA processes; implement full lot/material traceability; invest in sterilization compatibility testing (EtO, gamma, e‑beam) and environmental durability (vibration, thermal cycling, damp heat) per medical PCB norms; and replicate accelerated lifetime/soak testing to build a validation library—mirroring DYCONEX’s medtech posture ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Market Prospects – testing](https://www.market-prospects.com/articles/the-new-role-of-pcbs-in-smart-medical-devices)).

- Buy/Partner: Shorten time-to-medical credibility via (a) acquiring or partnering with a certified EMS/CDMO (ISO 13485, FDA registered) to anchor process validation and medical documentation flows; and (b) co‑developing electrode modules with a credentialed electrode OEM (e.g., as a substrate/HDI partner) to share biosignal/electrode chemistry expertise and regulatory experience during early implant programs. EMS/CDMOs explicitly market NPI‑to‑volume transfers, turnkey box‑builds, and regulatory credentials—useful scaffolds for LGIT’s medtech entry ([CO‑AX medical electronics](https://coaxinc.com/markets/medical-electronics/); [EMSG medical PCB](https://emsginc.com/industries-we-serve/medical-industry/); [GMI medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare)).

Quality/regulatory gap-closing actions (checklist)
- ISO 13485 certification with medical device documentation controls; supplier controls for medical‑grade materials (LCP/PI), noble metals, and coatings; UDI/labeling impacts if box‑build emerges. Demonstrate IPC-6013 Class III builds; publish whitepapers with adhesion, bend/flex, and soak test data akin to DYCONEX’s technical materials; and codify sterilization compatibility matrices (EtO, gamma) for offered stacks ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [MedDeviceOnline/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.meddeviceonline.com/doc/biocompatible-flexible-substrates-for-directly-implantable-applications-0002); [Medical Design Briefs LCP substrates](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/32810-using-lcp-based-flexible-substrates-for-medical-smart-sensor-applications)).

- Material/finish capability buildout: Capability to electroplate and pattern Au, Pt, PtIr with options for IrO2 electrode finishes; validate recessed/raised electrode geometries; stack‑ups with minimum ~50 µm substrate thickness as evidenced in med‑grade flex; demonstrate microvia reliability and fine‑line routing for high‑channel densities—benchmarked to DYCONEX specifications and LCP multilayer designs in literature ([MedDeviceOnline/DYCONEX whitepaper](https://www.meddeviceonline.com/doc/biocompatible-flexible-substrates-for-directly-implantable-applications-0002); [Medical Design Briefs LCP substrates](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/32810-using-lcp-based-flexible-substrates-for-medical-smart-sensor-applications)).

- Protective coatings and environmental robustness: Offer Parylene or equivalent medical‑grade protective films for body‑fluid isolation where applicable; document environmental testing suites (vibration, thermal cycling, damp heat) per medical PCB norms ([Market Prospects – coatings/testing](https://www.market-prospects.com/articles/the-new-role-of-pcbs-in-smart-medical-devices)).

Commercial templates and pricing posture
- Adopt standard medtech commercial frameworks: (1) Development phase: NRE for DFM, proto, fixtures, validation testing; (2) Pre‑production/pilot: milestone billing with yield/quality gates; (3) Production: volume‑tiered unit pricing, with SQE/quality metrics embedded; (4) Service: sustaining engineering and ECN management. EMS/CDMO narratives indicate OEM expectations for turnkey NPI→volume flow with transparent quality and collaboration rhythms, which LGIT should mirror in proposals and MSAs ([EMSG medical PCB](https://emsginc.com/industries-we-serve/medical-industry/); [GMI medical CDMO](https://www.gmisolutions.com/industries-served/focus-healthcare); [Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices)).

- Reference anchors: Use category scale (implantable electrodes $1.3B→$2.6B over 2026–2036; flexible implantable electrodes $2.9B in 2026) to frame capacity investment and portfolio strategy rather than to set SKU prices; absent public per‑unit benchmarks, focus on TCO reductions via yield, reliability, and scrap cost avoidance—key OEM selection drivers in regulated components ([Future Market Insights – market scale](https://www.futuremarketinsights.com/reports/implantable-neurostimulator-electrode-market); [ResearchAndMarkets – market scale](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOorsUWfpkV4twVdQwfGzbEpp_aechZZ-87xVUw5Xd99CPyuiPh2p)).

Risk mitigation and where not to start
- Do not begin with direct supply of chronic implant electrodes without ISO 13485 QMS, Class III evidence, sterilization compatibility data, and accelerated life/soak validation at hand; Quality/Regulatory would likely block. Instead, begin with external CGM/wearable interconnects and implant‑adjacent subassemblies while partnering for implant‑facing electrode learning—then graduate to implantable electrode substrates once validation dossiers are mature ([DYCONEX Medtech Implants](https://dyconex.com/medtech-implants); [Medical Design Briefs – evaluating ECMs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/29100-evaluating-electronics-contract-manufacturers-for-medical-devices)).

- Align internal roadmaps to therapy economics and adoption windows: Neurostimulation’s ongoing reimbursement (CPT 95970/95976/95977) supports sustained demand; BCI remains clinical/early commercial; CGM is scaled and partnership‑heavy—each demands a distinct cadence and engagement model ([Federal Register – CPT codes](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/07/16/2025-13271/medicare-and-medicaid-programs-cy-2026-payment-policies-under-the-physician-fee-schedule-and-other); [PR Newswire – CGM partnerships](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/global-continuous-glucose-monitoring-cgm-market-report-2021-2026-featuring-dexcom-abbott-laboratories-medtronic-and-glysens-301454481.html)).

Conclusion
For LG Innotek, the opportunity in CGM, neurostimulation, and BCI flexible substrates and electrode modules is sizable, but success depends on entering where its HDI capabilities translate into immediate OEM value under medtech process constraints. The defensible starting points are external CGM transmitter/patch interconnects and implant‑adjacent subassemblies—areas demanding ISO 13485, IPC Class III, traceability, and strong reliability documentation but stopping short of chronic implantation, while LGIT builds the material stacks (LCP/PI noble‑metal thin films; electrode finishes), sterilization compatibility, and accelerated life evidence required to expand into implantable electrode substrates. Partnering with credentialed EMS/CDMOs and electrode specialists will de‑risk regulatory/time-to‑market, while internal investments should concentrate on medical QMS, validation infrastructure, and material/finish capabilities that align with DYCONEX‑level benchmarks. Neurostimulation reimbursement stability, CGM partner ecosystems, and an expanding flexible implantable electrode market together signal sustained demand—yet the buying cycles are multi‑quarter to multi‑year and governed by rigorous cross‑functional DMUs. Positioning LGIT’s value proposition around validated quality, miniaturization, and reliability at scale—rather than commodity pricing—will be critical to win AVL approval and convert design-ins into long‑lived production awards.
